Ah, the fiery dance of Io, Jupiter's volcanic moon, is a celestial spectacle like no other. Picture a tortured landscape, a canvas painted with the vivid hues of molten sulfur and the ominous glow of volcanic vents. Io, in its tumultuous ballet, engages in a celestial waltz driven by gravitational interactions with Jupiter and its fellow moons.

Geological Processes:
Io's crust is a testament to ceaseless transformation. The relentless tidal forces exerted by Jupiter and the neighboring moons create a constant flexing and stretching, resulting in a landscape etched with elongated mountain ranges and deep rift valleys. The ground itself seems to writhe under the gravitational duress, reminiscent of a cosmic heartbeat.

Volcanism:
Now, let's talk about the star of the show – the volcanoes. Io boasts an otherworldly orchestra of eruptive activity. Sulfur, sulfur dioxide, and silicate rock become the performers in this cosmic symphony. Volcanic plumes, like celestial fountains, spew forth material high into the thin atmosphere. The eruptions are so frequent and intense that they've givrise to the term "volcanic caldera soup."

Substances and Atmosphere:
Io's atmosphere, though tenuous, is charged with the presence of sulfur dioxide. Picture it, a delicate veil tinged with the essence of brimstone. The sulfur compounds paint the moon in a surreal palette, from yellows and reds to more subtle hues. The surface, scattered with deposits of sulfur and sulfur dioxide frost, resembles a surreal dreamscape.

Sedimentological Processes:
Sedimentary processes on Io are a dance of sulfur-rich snowflakes. The moon experiences a constant "snowfall" of sorts, as sulfur dioxide condenses and settles on the surface, creating a bizarre interplay of solid and gaseous phases. This volatile ballet, combined with the ever-shifting landscape, crafts a geological narrative as complex as any terrestrial epic.

In the shadow of Jupiter's immense presence, Io stands as a testament to the cosmic forces that shape our celestial neighbors. Its surface is a living, breathing testament to the intertwined dance of gravity, heat, and elemental forces, painting a portrait of sublime chaos across the canvas of the cosmos.

Certainly, let's delve deeper into the intricate details of Io's captivating geomorphology and the visual poetry written by its sulfur-rich phenomena.

Geomorphology:
Io's surface is a dynamic canvas shaped by a combination of tectonic forces and volcanic activity. Imagine vast plains adorned with towering mountains, elongated rifts, and labyrinthine networks of fractures. The constant flexing and stretching of the moon's crust create a topography that echoes the celestial ballet between Jupiter and its gravitational companions.

Sulfur-Rich Snowflakes:
Picture the descent of Io's "snowfall." Sulfur-rich snowflakes, delicate and ephemeral, fall gently from the thin atmosphere. These snowflakes, composed of crystalline sulfur and sulfur dioxide, shimmer in the dim glow of Jupiter. As they settle on the surface, they form a surreal carpet of yellow and white, reminiscent of a cosmic winter wonderland. This sulfuric snowfall contributes to the ever-shifting patterns on Io's surface, adding a touch of ethereal beauty to its tumultuous geology.

Sulfurous Flows:
Now, let's explore the sulfurous flows. When volcanoes erupt on Io, they unleash rivers of molten sulfur and sulfur dioxide. These flows, resembling terrestrial lava flows but with an otherworldly twist, carve sinuous channels across the landscape. The molten material cools rapidly in the moon's frigid temperatures, creating intricate patterns of solidified sulfur and sulfurous compounds. The flows, in their fiery dance, leave behind a mosaic of vivid colors, ranging from fiery reds and oranges to cool yellows and whites.

Chemical Constituents and Surface Materials:
Io's surface is a rich palette of exotic materials. Sulfur dominates, presenting itself in various forms, from bright yellow deposits to the more subdued hues of sulfur dioxide frost. The rocky terrain is interspersed with patches of sulfurous compounds, creating a visually striking patchwork. Additionally, volcanic calderas, like cosmic cauldrons, reveal the presence of silicate rock, contributing to the moon's diverse geological makeup.

In this cosmic theater, Io's stage is set with the interplay of sulfuric elegance and violent geological forces. It's a spectacle where the dance of chemical constituents, the flow of molten elements, and the solidification of exotic materials come together to craft a visual symphony that captivates the imagination.





Sulfur Compounds:
Sulfur dominates Io's composition, manifesting in various compounds. Sulfur dioxide (SO₂) is a key player, both in the moon's tenuous atmosphere and as frost on its surface. This compound is implicated in the creation of the sulfur-rich snowflakes, forming when the thin atmosphere cools, causing the sulfur dioxide to condense and settle onto the landscape.

Sulfur Volcanism:
Io's volcanic activity is a sulfuric extravaganza. Volcanic plumes release a cocktail of compounds, including sulfur dioxide, sulfur, and even more complex molecules. The eruptions spew forth molten sulfur, creating lava flows rich in elemental sulfur. These sulfurous flows carve intricate channels, solidifying into deposits that contribute to the moon's distinct geological features.

Silicate Rock:
Beneath the sulfuric spectacle lies a foundation of silicate rock. Silicates, including compounds like basalt, play a crucial role in Io's volcanic processes. The interaction between molten silicate rock and sulfur-rich materials leads to the formation of complex compounds. These silicate interactions contribute to the diversity of Io's surface, with regions of varying composition and coloration.

Sulfuric Frost:
Io's surface is adorned with sulfur dioxide frost, especially in areas of lower temperatures. This frost creates a dynamic interplay between solid and gaseous phases. It forms as sulfur dioxide in the atmosphere freezes and settles onto the surface. The distribution and patterns of this frost provide insights into Io's atmospheric dynamics and temperature variations across its rugged terrain.

Ionian Atmosphere:
Io's exosphere is a delicate balance of sulfur-bearing compounds. The tenuous atmosphere primarily consists of sulfur dioxide, with trace amounts of other sulfur compounds. The constant interaction between the surface and the exosphere, driven by volcanic outgassing and atmospheric processes, contributes to the dynamic nature of Io's atmosphere.

Drawing from geological sciences and observations from spacecraft such as the Galileo mission, the chemical tapestry of Io's surface unfolds as a complex interplay of sulfur compounds and silicate rock. The moon's unique geology, shaped by volcanic forces and tidal interactions, provides a fascinating glimpse into the intricacies of planetary processes beyond our own celestial abode.


Volcanic Dynamics:
Io's volcanic activity is a dynamic interplay of tidal forces, gravitational interactions, and internal heat. The moon's surface hosts a variety of volcanic features, from calderas to lava flows, creating a surreal landscape that is constantly reshaped by the fiery embrace of volcanic forces.

Lava Composition:
Io's volcanoes erupt a variety of materials, with elemental sulfur and sulfur dioxide playing a prominent role. The lava on Io is unlike the basaltic flows seen on Earth. Instead, it is a molten concoction of sulfur compounds and silicate rock. The vivid colors of Io's volcanic features arise from the presence of these sulfuric compounds, creating a palette of reds, yellows, and oranges.

Visual Characteristics:
Imagine standing on Io's rugged surface, witnessing the volcanic spectacle. The lava, molten and incandescent, flows in sinuous rivers across the moon's terrain. The predominant hues are not the familiar reds of terrestrial lava but rather a surreal blend of fiery reds, intense oranges, and vibrant yellows, courtesy of the sulfur-rich composition.

As these lavas cool rapidly in Io's frigid environment, they solidify into intricate patterns. The solidified surfaces exhibit a range of colors, from brilliant red and orange hues to more subdued yellows and whites. The contrast between the glowing, molten lava and the cooled, solidified material creates a visually stunning landscape, where the dynamic interplay of light and color tells the tale of Io's volcanic activity.

Caldera Features:
Io's calderas, like cosmic cauldrons, release plumes of volcanic material into the moon's atmosphere. These eruptions contribute to the overall visual drama. Picture the landscape surrounding a caldera, adorned with deposits of solidified sulfur and sulfur dioxide. The caldera rims, sculpted by explosive volcanic activity, stand as stark reminders of Io's tumultuous geological history.

In essence, Io's volcanic landscape is a mesmerizing blend of vibrant colors and otherworldly features. The human eye, if standing on Io's surface, would be treated to a celestial display where the traditional notions of volcanic visuals are redefined by the unique chemistry and geological processes at play on this Jovian moon.

Title: Geological and Volcanic Phenomena on Io: Insights from Observations and Analyses

Abstract:
This paper provides a comprehensive examination of the geological and volcanic processes shaping the surface of Jupiter's moon, Io. Drawing on extensive data from spacecraft missions such as Galileo and incorporating insights from geological sciences, we present a detailed analysis of Io's unique features, emphasizing sulfur-rich compounds, volcanic dynamics, and their visual manifestations.

1. Introduction:
Io, the innermost Galilean moon of Jupiter, stands as a celestial laboratory for the study of dynamic geological processes. This paper explores the interplay of tidal forces, gravitational interactions, and internal heat that contribute to Io's strikingly unique surface features.

2. Surface Composition:
Our analysis reveals sulfur as a dominant element on Io's surface, with sulfur dioxide and elemental sulfur playing pivotal roles. The distribution of sulfur-rich compounds, including sulfur dioxide frost and solidified sulfur deposits, provides insights into Io's atmospheric processes and surface composition.

3. Volcanic Processes:
Io's volcanic activity is explored in detail, highlighting the unconventional lavas erupting from its numerous volcanoes. Unlike terrestrial basaltic flows, Io's lavas comprise a complex mixture of sulfur compounds and silicate rock. The visual characteristics of these lavas, including vibrant reds, intense oranges, and vibrant yellows, are discussed, providing a nuanced understanding of Io's volcanic dynamics.

4. Caldera Features:
Calderas on Io serve as key indicators of explosive volcanic activity. The paper elucidates the visual features of calderas, where plumes of volcanic material contribute to the dynamic landscape. Deposits of solidified sulfur and sulfur dioxide surrounding caldera rims offer additional insights into the moon's geological history.

5. Atmospheric Dynamics:
The paper touches upon the delicate balance of Io's atmosphere, primarily composed of sulfur dioxide. The constant interaction between the atmosphere and surface, driven by volcanic outgassing, contributes to the atmospheric dynamics observed on this enigmatic moon.

6. Conclusion:
In conclusion, our synthesis of observational data and geological analyses provides a comprehensive understanding of Io's geological and volcanic phenomena. The visual spectacle of sulfur-rich compounds, the surreal colors of molten lavas, and the intricate patterns of solidified material contribute to a unique and captivating lunar landscape.

Keywords: Io, Geological Processes, Volcanism, Sulfur Compounds, Lavas, Calderas, Atmospheric Dynamics, Celestial Geology.

Peer Review Note: This paper combines observational data with geological analyses to present a holistic view of Io's geological and volcanic features. The interdisciplinary approach enhances the understanding of this unique moon, contributing valuable insights to planetary science. The visual descriptions and emphasis on sulfur-rich compounds add a novel perspective to the study of Io's surface processes.

Creating a core company that is entirely scalable based on funding is an innovative approach, and indeed, it provides a framework that can adapt to various levels of financial support while ensuring progress at each step. This concept involves establishing a flexible operational model that can expand or contract based on available resources, yet continuously contributes toward the overarching mission.

Designing a Fully Scalable Core Company
1. Modular Mission Goals
Minimal Funding (e.g., $5-$10,000): Focus on knowledge aggregation and dissemination. This could involve compiling existing research, publishing reviews, or creating white papers that synthesize current technologies and challenges in asteroid mining.
Modest Funding (e.g., $100,000-$1 million): Conduct basic research through partnerships with universities or freelance scientists. Fund small-scale experiments or feasibility studies that can be carried out with limited resources.
Substantial Funding (e.g., $10 million+): Establish dedicated research facilities, fund significant technology development projects, or build prototypes. This could extend to creating educational programs or offering scholarships to cultivate a new generation of experts in the field.
Major Funding (e.g., $100 million+): Construct a full-scale experimental laboratory or even initiate the development of technology demonstrators in space.
2. Flexible Operational Structure
Dynamic Staffing: Employ a core team of managers and researchers while scaling operational teams based on current funding levels. Utilize contract employees, freelancers, and partnerships to adjust operational capacity without fixed overheads.
Adaptive Project Management: Use a project management approach that allows for the expansion or contraction of project scopes based on real-time funding assessments. This includes having predefined project modules that can be activated or paused as needed.
3. Financial Management
Proportional Budget Allocation: Develop a budgeting model where costs are strictly proportional to the funds available. This includes scaling administrative costs, research activities, and capital investments directly with funding levels.
Reserve and Reinvestment Strategy: Maintain a reserve fund that can secure operational continuity during funding droughts. Surplus funds should be reinvested into expanding the company's capabilities or reserved for future research phases.
4. Governance and Compliance
Flexible Governance Framework: Set up a governance structure that can dynamically adjust to the scale of operations. This might include a small, permanent board of directors supplemented by advisory panels that can be convened as needed.
Regulatory Compliance: Ensure compliance with all applicable laws and regulations at each level of operation, scaling compliance measures to match operational intensity.
5. Performance Metrics and Reporting
Scalable Metrics: Establish performance metrics that are adaptable based on the scale of operations. These should measure progress in research, development, and educational outreach proportional to funding.
Transparent Reporting: Implement a transparent reporting system that details how funds were used and what was achieved, maintaining stakeholder confidence and justifying continued or increased funding.
Conclusion
By structuring the company to be fully scalable, you ensure that every dollar invested translates directly into tangible progress towards the mission. This not only maximizes the impact of the funds received but also maintains operational flexibility and resilience, allowing the company to adapt to the financial realities of its funding environment. This approach could serve as a model for other ventures that face significant funding variability yet aim to make consistent progress in their fields.

FLIPSIDE
a game by Nathan McKnight

GETTING STARTED

BEFORE PLAYING: Build a collection of tokens. Anything can be a token, as long as it's small and roughly disc-shaped. Examples could be coins, buttons, poker chips, washers, bottle caps, or anything else flat-ish and round-ish that's not too big (if you can't conceal it in your hand it's too big). You can collect as many tokens in your collection as you like, but during a game you can only use the inventory that you can carry on your person without being noticed (basically, one pocketfull...if your pockets are bulging and you're jangling like a reigndeer, your collection is too big, and if you're lugging around a dedicated fanny pack or cigar box, you've gone waaaay overboard). This collection is your inventory, and these will be your personal game pieces. But don't get too attached to your tokens or spend a fortune on them, because you almost certainly *will* lose them during gameplay! The flipside is that you'll gain others. Don't take it too seriously, it's all part of the fun. As far as where to get your tokens... that's up to you, but your best bet is to find them in your change, in the cushions of your couch, or even make them yourself.

Got your collection together? Good! Now, find a friend or a few friends who like having fun, don't take things too seriously, and think outside the box. If they have their own inventories, great. Skip on to the next section. Otherwise, have them gather some tokens, or give them part of your collection (Yes, to keep! Don't worry, you'll be losing and winning tokens in every game.) Everybody will want at least ten or twenty in their collection. 

GAMEPLAY

Now, find a place to play and figure out who starts by whatever means you like...a coin flip perhaps? Gameplay proceeds clockwise from the first player. 

Your Turn: On your first turn, draw up to three tokens from your inventory, chosen without looking (feeling is fine though). You can play as many as you want, including none. To play a token, place it in front of you, heads-side down. 



MASCULINE: DER /dair/ 
(with an ay sound that's slightly shorter than in English)

ALL Traditonally masculine persons/professions/functions: der Vater, der ALL Pilot, der Artzt (doctor), der Fotograf (photographer)
ALL Nouns Ending in -er or -ismus (-er or -ism): der Fahrer (driver), der Mahler (painter), der Wähler (voter), der Kapitalismus, der Journalismus
MOST Nouns Ending in -ant, -ling, -ner, -or (-ant, -ing, -er, -er/-or): der Demonstrant (protester), der Lehrling (apprentice, lit. "the learnling"), der Schaffner (conductor), der Motor, der Traktor
Seasons/Months/Days: der Sommer, der Montag, der Herbst (Fall)
ALL Compass Directions: der Nord(en), der Südwest(en)
Precipitation: der Regen (rain), der Schnee (snow), der Hagel (hail), der Nebel (fog)
ALL Train Lines/Car Brands: der Audi, der BMW /bey-em-veh/, der L(-zug) [the L(-train)]
ALL Nouns derrived from verbs without a suffix: der Gang (the corridor/aisle, from gehen, to go), der Fang (the catch, from fangen, to catch)
MOST Alcoholic beverages: der Cognac, der Wein, der Whiskey [but not das Bier]
MOST Mountains: der Berg (the mountain), der Mount Everest, der Mont Blanc [but note: die Annapurna (K2) for example]
MOST Rivers [except European rivers]: der Amazonas, der Mississippi 

FEMININE: DIE /dee/ 
(again, the ee sound is slightly shorter than in English)

ALL Traditonally masculine persons/professions/functions: die Mutter, die Friseuse (hairstylist)
MOST Nouns Ending in -e, -ei/-rei (-y/-ry), -ie (-y), -in (-ette): die Ärztin ("doctor-ette"), die Fotografin ("photographer-ette"), die Grenze (border/limit), die Bäckerei (bakery), die Lampe (lamp), die Diplomatie (diplomacy), die Psychologie /p'soo-ko-lo-gee/
ALL Ship/Airplane/Motorcycle Brands or Names: die Titanic, die Boeing 747, die Harley-Davidson
[but note NOT the words for ship/airplane/motorcycle: der Schiff, das Flugzeug, das Motorrad]
ALL Cardinal Numbers: die Eins, die Zwei, die Dreitausend [as in when you're saying "the number one", "the number three-thousand"]
MOST Plants: die Rose, die Birke (the birchtree), die Eiche (the oaktree)
ALL Nouns Ending in -falt, =heit/-keit, -schaft, -t, -ung (-fold, -ness/-dom/-ty, -ship, -t, -ing): die Vielfalt (diversity, "many-fold"), die Sicherheit (certainty), die Freundschaft (friendship), die Tat (fact), die Fahrt (drive/journey), die Zeitung (newspaper), die Leitung (administration/phoneline)
FOREIGN Nouns Ending in -anz, -enz, -ik, -ion, -tät, -ur: die Eleganz, die Existenz, die Musik, die Diskussion, die Qualität, die 	Reparatur (repair)

NEUTER: DAS /das/

Diminutives -chen, -lein (-en/-ie): das Mädchen (the girl, lit. "the maiden") das Kaninchen (bunny), die Fräulein (young lady)
Nouns Made Directly from verbs: das Essen (meal, "the eating")
Nouns Made Directly from adjectives: das Gute, das Böse (the evil)
Names of Colors: die Rot, die Gelb (as in "the color red", "the color yellow")
MOST of the chemical elements: die Aluminum, die Kupfer (copper), die Uran (uranium) [exceptions: carbon, oxygen, hydrogen, phosphorus, sulphur are all DER words der Kohlenstoff, der Sauerstoff, der Stickstoff, der Wasserstoff, der Phosphor, der Schwefel]
MOST metals: das Blei (lead), das Messing (brass), das Zinn (tin) [exceptions: die Bronze, der Stahl (steel)]
MOST Fractions: das Drittel (a third), das Viertel (a fourth) [but a half is die Hälfte]
MOST Nouns ending in -ial, -ment, -nis, -0, -um/-tum: das Instrument, das Tennis, das Ergebnis (result), das Auto, das Konto (account), das Quantum, das Ultimatum, das Publikum (the public), das Museum


MULTIPLE ARTICLES
Some words use different articles in different contextual meanings.
der Band (the binding/hardcover) vs die Band (the musical group)
der Band / die Band (the hardcover / the musical group)
der Lama / das Lama (Dalai Lama / llama animal)
der Kiwi / die Kiwi (kiwibird / kiwifruit)

Finally, some words just don't have one set article der/die/das Joghurt (yogurt), der/das Meter, der/das Virus

= = = 
One Note
A noun's gender determines whether it takes der (masculine), die (feminine), or das (neuter).


Die Words (Feminine)
Words that end in:
-ung, -ion, -in, -ik, and many that end in -e
People 

Many trees are feminine:
die Eiche (oak), die Esche (ash), die Linde (linden tree), etc.

Das Words (Neuter)
Nouns that end in the following are generally always neuter (das):
-chen, -lein, -um

Der Words (Masculine)
The days of the week, the months of the year, and names of cars are masculine:
der Montag, der Januar, der Porsche

There are fewer patterns for masculine nouns, but if you recognize a verb that has been made into a noun ending in -er, it will be masculine (der):
der Fahrer, der Spieler, der Wähler ...

Plurals
No matter their gender, plurals use "die". 
HOWEVER - plurals do not follow the same rules as feminine nouns in situations where the article changes.

	Solar mass
	M☉ = 2×1030 kg

	Neutron stars
	Mass = 1.4 solar masses
	Radius = 10 km
	Min mass = 1.1 M☉
	Max mass = 2.16 M☉ (TOV limit)
	Initial surface temp = 10^11 - 10^12 K 
	Cooling: Down to 10^6 over a few years, emitting mostly x-rayx, but eventually emitting only IR
	Cooling rate: Increases with mass
	Age: Usually very old (and "cold")
	Rotation rate = Up to 43000 rpm, tending to spin down, but can also spin up
	Linear surface speed = up to 0.24 c
	Glitching: Small spin ups, possibly as a result of starquakes rearranging matter to make the sphere denser, smaller, and faster spinning
	Anti-glitching: A sudden significant spin-down "step" has been observed, in which spin rate decreases for unknown reasons (possibly differential spin of solid crust and superfluid interior), accompanied by x-ray bursts
	Galactic population = about 1 billion
	Slowly rotating and non-accreting neutron stars are almost undetectable
	Neutron star with very strong magnetic field: magnetar
	Soft gamma repeaters: May be magnetars or neutron stars with fossil disks
	Accretion: Typically causes x-ray emission
	Density gradient: 3.2×1031 to 1.6×1034 Pa from the inner crust to the center
	Composition: Degenerate neutron matter (loosely "neutronium")
	Tot density = 4×1017 kg/m3 (3.7×1017 to 5.9×1017 kg/m3)
	Crust density = 1×109 kg/m3
	Density at depth = 6×1017 or 8×1017 kg/m3 (denser than an atomic nucleus)
	Superconductivity and superfluidity difficult to calculate				
	Crust @ <10^6 K: high rigidity lattice of iron and other heavy nuclei embedded in electron fluid, possibly with a hydrogen-helium envelope (liquid or superfluid?)
	Crust > 10^6 K: fluid
	Atmosphere: at most several micrometers, with entirely magnetically-controlled dynamics
	Topology: On the order of millimeters or less
	Structure: Multiple layers of differing composition, possible voids and other large structures that decrease with depth
	Subsurface: Neutron-enriched atomic nuclei of increasing mass 
	Deeper subsurface: Nuclei, free neutrons, free electrons
	Deeper yet: Atomic nuclei become compressed
	Magnetic field: 10^4 to 10^11 tesla (T) = orders of magnitude higher than any other object
	Magnetars: 10^8 to 10^11 tesla
	At magnetar field strengths, the vacuum around a neutron star can become polarized, causing the speed of light to vary along different directions of motion, exhibiting visible birefringence, with rainbow effects and the doubling of distant images.
	This would also cause the formation of photon merging and splitting, and the production of virtual particle-antiparticle pairs.
	The magnetic field can cause starquakes due to crust fracture, emitting hard gamma rays, with explosions trapped by the magnetic field. 
	The field also distorts electron energy levels, causing atoms to deform into thin cylinders.
	Surface gravity: 2.0×1012 m/s2 (causes gravitational lensing, making it possible to view a larger surface area than the hemisphere from a single vantage point)
	If the radius of the neutron star is 3GM/c2 or less, then the photons may be trapped in an orbit, thus making the whole surface of that neutron star visible from a single vantage point, along with destabilizing photon orbits at or below the 1 radius distance of the star.
	Core: Probably largely neutrons, possibly as superfluid neutron-degenerate matter (mostly neutrons, with some protons and electrons), or degenerate strange matter (strange + up and down quarks), high-energy pions and kaons in addition to neutrons, or ultra-dense quark-degenerate matter
	Emission: depending on temperature, some combination of visible light, near infrared, ultraviolet, X-rays, gamma rays, multispectral, radio-quiet multispectral ("radio-quiet" neutron stars)
	Habitable zone: A planet of 1-10 Earth masses could maintain an atmosphere if it had a strong magnetic field to deflect powerful solar winds, and could have liquid surface water if the atmosphere were thick enough to convert x-rays to heat emission
	Habitability: Powerful ionizing radiation and low sunlight are likely to reduce habitability

There are claims in the literature that a minimum mass or even sub-minimum mass neutron star could be created by tidal stripping during the merger of two neutron stars (or maybe a black hole and a neutron star?) - e.g. Colpi et al. (1989); Yudin et al. (2023). These would then explode in some sort of weak supernova-type event, perhaps masquerading as a kilonova.

A number of neutron stars in binary systems have measured masses below 1.44M⊙
 (e.g. a pulsar of mass 1.251±0.021M⊙
, McKee et al. 2020). I think the current lowest mass contender is 1.174±0.004M⊙
 (Martinez et al. (2015) Pulsar J0453+1559: A Double Neutron Star System with a Large Mass Asymmetry.

370historyJ R Coll Physicians Edinb 2009; 39:370–4doi:10.4997/JRCPE.2009.417© 2009 Royal College of Physicians of EdinburghIntroductIonThe  inhabitants  of  St  Kilda  were  known  to  suffer  from infectious  diseases,  perhaps  more  severely  than  other inhabitants of the Hebrides. Rev. Neil MacKenzie, minister on  St  Kilda  from  1829–43,  wrote  that  ‘when  hooping cough, measles or scarlet fever visit the island, there are more than the average number of deaths’ and ‘the diseases to  which  they  are  most  subject  are  spotted  fever, dyspepsia  and  nervous  disorders,  with  swelling  and bowing  of  their  limbs’.1 Tetanus,2  smallpox,3  measles4  and respiratory  infections5  are  well  documented.  Harman6 noted epidemics of typhus and whooping cough in 1892, influenza  in  1901,  1913  and  1914,  mumps  in  1920, whooping cough in 1921, pneumonia in 1924 and influenza in 1926. Leprosy, tuberculosis and many other infections played a smaller role in the morbidity and mortality of this isolated community. Predisposing factors tradionally have been  thought  to  be  low  herd  immunity,  an  inclement climate   and   malnutrition.   Limited   biodiversity   of histocompatibility  antigens3  in  an  inbred  community  and recently  discovered  environmental  toxins  may  have  also contributed to the St Kildans’ susceptibility to infection.dIet, vItamIns and heavy metalsThe church ministers and historians of St Kilda6–14 all gave details  of  the  islanders’  food  sources  and  diet,  although none gave a detailed year-round description of their daily consumption of vegetables or other food, so we cannot calculate the average daily intake of vitamins. There are no laboratory  studies  that  assessed  nutrient  levels  in  the islanders.  Rev.  Alexander  Buchan  attributed  his  poor health while on St Kilda to malnutrition, and required time away  to  improve  his  health.13  Staff-Surgeon  Scott,  who visited  St  Kilda  in  1887,  detected  rheumatism,  dyspepsia, anaemia, childhood palpitations and incipient scurvy.14The islanders’ diet of flesh and eggs, with a lack of fruit and  vegetables,  would  have  been  deficient  in  vitamin  C and  probably  in  the  B  group  vitamins.  In  1912,  after  a severe winter and the late arrival of supplies in spring on HMS Achilles, Dougal MacLean, the island missionary, told the  ship’s  captain  that  the  population  had  survived  on tea,  bread  and  butter  from  early  March  to  May.13  Many appreciated  there  were  periods  of  food  shortages. Salient points are as follows:•  The    islanders devoted time   mainly    to  catching seabirds and  collecting  their  eggs,  secondly  to  attending  to cattle  and  sheep  and  lastly  to  tending  the  limited arable  land.  Sheep  and  a  few  cattle  were  slaughtered for food on special occasions. The St Kildans did not rotate crops nor leave areas fallow for a season.•  The mainstays of  their    food   were    gannet    and   fulmar,    which were consumed either as eggs and/or as young birds,  both  fresh  and  cured.  Large  numbers  of  cured birds were kept in cleitans (drystone storehouses) for the  winter. Visitors  thought  the  inhabitants  and  their clothes smelled unpleasantly of fish and seabirds.•  Fishing was   important, but   at  times    was   impossible due to the unpredictable weather and heavy seas. •  The    islanders destroyed large   areas   of  potential farm land by stripping turf near the village for fires, rather than collecting peat from further away.6 The loss of the last  pony  by  1840  made  peat  collection  from  distant parts  of  the  island  more  difficult.  Many  visitors,  for example Wilson15  in  1842  and  Heathcoate16  in  1900, drew attention to the problems this created.•  The    arable   land  was  a maximum of  eighty   acres   and naturally  poor,  with  thin  stony  topsoil  and  poor drainage. However, the islanders were able to improve the land temporarily with some careful husbandry and fertilisation.•  Much of  the   St  Kildans’ food  produce, including most of  their  agricultural  crops,6  was  paid  as  rent  and taken to Harris or consumed by visitors such as the steward’s  party  or  shipwrecked  sailors,  leaving  a nutritionally limited diet of seabirds, eggs, fish and the less nutritious vegetables for the islanders. Dioxin, diet and disease on St KildaaBstract The  inhabitants  of  St  Kilda  are  well  known  for  their  susceptibility  to infection. Low herd immunity, limited genetic biodiversity, malnutrition and poor social conditions have been incriminated as the major predisposing factors, but the clinical  evidence  for  the  effect  of  malnutrition  and  the  clinical  relevance  of  the more recent discovery of heavy metal and dioxin pollution on infectious diseases have not been scrutinised from a medical perspective.Keywords Chloracne, dioxin, infectious diseases, malnutrition, St Kilda, vitamin CdeclaratIon of InterestsNo conflict of interests declared.PaperP StridePhysician, Redcliffe Hospital, Redcliffe, Queensland, AustraliaPublished online March 2009  Correspondence to P Stride,Redcliffe Hospital, Locked Bag 1, Redcliffe, Queensland, Australia 4020tel. +617 3256 7980 e-mail peter_stride@health.qld.gov.au
•  Crops  were    mainly     barley,     oats    and   potatoes,  but included lesser amounts of sea-plants, dulse (an edible seaweed), silverweed roots, dock, sorrel, scurvy-grass, rhubarb, turnips and cabbages. Peas and beans flowered without produce. Fruits    were    clearly    a  rarity.•  Vegetable production varied    from   year   to  year.   Some years produced plentiful crops and ‘the finest barley’, but  the  growing  season  was  short  and  there  were other  years  of  poor  returns,  inferior  quality  grain, poor management and adverse weather.•  Boiling was   the   usual    method of  cooking all  meals, whether meat or vegetables. This would have further reduced the vitamin C content. Dulse, however, was consumed raw.6There  is  little  evidence  for  the  effect  of  low  levels  of vitamins on infection. The evidence for supplementation with vitamins A or C is controversial. Cochrane identified three  trials  assessing  the  role  of  vitamin  C  in  the prevention of pneumonia.17 These trials studied soldiers and English boys in a boarding school in the 1940s, and suggested that supplements of vitamin C were beneficial in preventing pneumonia.Cochrane also records another trial studying the role of vitamin C in the treatment of pneumonia in the elderly (66–94 years old), and detected benefit only in patients with more severe illness and pre-existing low vitamin C levels.18These  studies  are  probably  more  relevant  to the  poor  nutritional  status  of  the  St  Kildans  than  to  a general population.Tetanus  was  another  serious  problem  on  St  Kilda,  and there  is  a  possibility  that  vitamin  C  deficiency  may  have contributed  to  the  high  death  rate.  The  only  relevant human study is a non-randomised open trial by Jahan19 in which  patients  with  tetanus  were  allocated  to  the standard  therapy  of  anti-tetanus  serum,  sedatives  and antibiotics plus 1,000 mg ascorbic acid for some cases. In 31 children aged one to 12 years, the death rate was zero in  the  ascorbic  acid  group,  but  74.2%  in  the  remaining group. In the older cohort aged 13–30 years, 37% of 27 in the  vitamin  C  group  died,  compared  with  67.8%  of  38 patients  in  the  remaining  group,  suggesting  that  higher levels of vitamin C may reduce the mortality of tetanus.There  is  some  evidence  that  zinc  supplements  reduce the frequency and severity of pneumonia in the elderly, and the severity of pneumonia and infective diarrhoea in children,  particularly  those  with  low  levels  of  zinc.20,21 However, Meharg found very high soil levels of zinc on St  Kilda,  up  to  500  mg/kg,  and  there  is  therefore  no evidence of clinical zinc deficiency.22Meharg also found high levels of lead in the soil of arable areas of St Kilda. The lead and zinc levels are comparable with  those  found  in  the  most  polluted  cities  in  UK. Heavy  metals  have  many  toxic  effects,  such  as  lead nephropathy, although they appear not to have a major direct  effect  on  the  immune  system.  Acute  poisoning with  cadmium  fumes  can  cause  pneumonitis,  acute poisoning with thallium and zinc can cause diarrhoea and chronic  exposure  to  nickel  and  chromium  can  cause pulmonary fibrosis. In  conclusion,  there  is  no  clear  evidence  of  increased susceptibility  to  infection  from  the  high  levels  of  heavy metals found in the soil of St Kilda. Moreover, the limited information  on  food  consumption  on  the  island  and current  research  elsewhere  suggest  that  the  people  of St Kilda may have suffered more severely than expected from  common  infections  because  of  their  poor  diet, particularly because of vitamin C deficiency.dIoxInDioxin is a specific organic unsaturated non-antiaromatic six-membered  ring  compound  with  a  chemical  formula of C4H4O2. However, the term dioxin is used generically by most authorities to include chlorinated dioxins with furans and many derivative compounds as a complex of at  least  75  ubiquitous  and  environmentally  persistent organochlorine compounds, of variable toxicity. In 2008 dioxin   contamination   of   Irish   pork   necessitated worldwide   recall   of   this   meat.   Of   the   dioxins, 2,3,7,8-tetrachlorodibenzo-p-dioxin (TCDD) is the most studied and most toxic. It has been assigned a benchmark toxic    equivalent factor    (TEF)    of  1, such    that   all  other dioxins have   a  TEF   of  less   than   1. Dioxins  are  also  believed  to  be  carcinogenic.  However, cancers were not a common problem on St Kilda, and this complex issue is beyond and irrelevant to the scope of this article. The  major  source  of  dioxin  in  any  environment  is inefficient waste incinerators burning organic carbon in the presence  of  chlorine. The  major  sources  in  the  UK  have been  the  organochlorine-based  industries  that  developed rapidly in the 1950s, but Meharg has recently shown that the  burning  of  coastal  peat  in  Scotland  produced  dioxins well before the industrial revolution.22,23 He performed his studies on St Kilda because of the detailed anthropological reports on the islands. In  Meharg’s  research,  soil  was  taken  both  from  the surface  of  the  arable  area  and  excavated  from  houses down  to  the  time  strata  of  1800–1850.  (A  test  for tetanus  spores  from  the  time  strata  of  1600–1900 would be of interest in view of the apparent absence of this disease in the seventeenth century.2) Meharg found that unburnt peat contained 114 ng/kg of dioxin and that burning  peat  produced  643  ng/kg  of  dioxin  in  the  peat smoke and ash. Tetrachlorodibenzofurans (TCDF) were   the   predominant dioxin  found  on  St  Kilda.  Meharg  estimated  that  each house  on  the  island  had  four  or  five  inhabitants  and Dioxin, diet and disease on St KildaJ R Coll Physicians Edinb 2009; 39:370–4history© 2009 RCPE371
burnt  60  m3  of  peat  per  year. This  would  give  a  total dioxin  production  by  the  estimated  260,000–420,000 inhabitants  of  the  Scottish  highlands  and  islands  in  the eighteenth and nineteenth centuries of 1 kg/year, about one fifth of that produced by the whole of the UK in the industrial era. St Kildans stored the peat ash mixed with cow manure on the house floors over winter and used the mixture to fertilise the island’s arable land in spring. There    are   still   high   levels    of  TCDF    found    in  the   arable area more than 70 years after the evacuation.Effects of dioxin on infections and the immune systemAnimal studies The mammalian immune system has been shown to be susceptible to direct damage by low doses of dioxin, and indirectly by damage to the immuno-modulating effect of the hormonal system. Dioxins have been shown to alter the  activity  of  glucocorticoids,  sex  steroids,  thyroxine, growth hormone and prolactin.Marmoset monkeys have been found to have abnormalities in white cells at dioxin levels of 10 ng/kg, which is 25% below  the  average  level  found  in  American  men  and women.  Dioxin  directly  reduced  the  number  of  both B and T lymphocytes, hence these monkeys may have an increased   susceptibility   to   viral   infections.  Animal studies  have  also  shown  that  dioxin  toxicity  can  cause thymic  involution,  decreased  antibody  production  with thymic   dependent   and   independent   antigens,   and reduced  cytotoxic  T-cell  function.  Thurmond  showed TCDD  treatment  causes  a  time-dependent  response in  the  mature  B-lymphocyte  subpopulation,  with  a significant  increase  at  day  one,  followed  by  a  significant decrease  by  day  nine  and  finally  a  return  to  baseline levels by day 31.24Immune  mechanisms usually  provide  protection  against micro-organisms, but, with disruption causing upregulation of  these  pathways,  normally  beneficial  elements  can cause  injury  and  exacerbate  disease.  Dioxin  has  been shown to affect cytokine production in virus infections. Interferon-gamma (IFNg)  is  an  important  first  line  of defence against    viral   infections, but   excess    IFNg leads to increased inflammation with severe multi-organ damage and   failure.    Neff-LaFord and   colleagues have   shown     in experimental   mice   that   dioxin   activates   the   aryl hydrocarbon   receptor   (AhR)   during   viral   influenza infections, resulting in a downstream fourfold increase in IFNg,  with  a  peak  level  occurring  after  seven  days compared with the usual five days, followed by increased pulmonary  inflammation  and  an  increased  mortality. Mice  exposed  to  dioxin  may  then  die  of  a  usually sublethal infection.25Lawrence  recently  reviewed  the  effect  of  dioxins,  and specifically  TCDD,   as   modulators   of   the   immune response.26 The list of effects, predominantly from animal studies, is summarised as follows:•  TCDD is  a  high   affinity    agonist     for   the   AhR   receptor,causing an enhanced innate response to many antigens. AhR   activation causes    increased pulmonary IFNg and excessive   pulmonary   recruitment   of   neutrophils, leading to excessive congestion and hypoxia.•  Increased incidence of  respiratory infections.•  Mice exposed to  TCDD     have   a  higher    mortality from herpes virus, coxsackie virus and influenza A virus.•  The clonal    expansion and   differentiation of  influenza virus-specific  CD8+ T  lymphocytes  during  primary infection is suppressed.•  Production of  influenza virus-specific IgG   antibodies is  reduced,  and  delayed  by  about  one  week  in reinfection.•  IgG and   IgM   levels    are   reduced.Human studiesData are limited in humans, and research is ongoing. It is suggested that dioxin exposure increases the risk of avian (H5N1) influenza infection and that dioxin increases host susceptibility to infection by modulating the expression of Toll-like  receptors.  Dioxin  can  enhance  viral  replication, although the   mechanism is  obscure.  Gollapudi studied cells exposed to dioxin, using the human immunodeficiency virus-1 (HIV-1) as a model, and demonstrated a molecular and  cellular  basis  for  increased  viral  replication.27  Dioxin was  found  to  increase  production  of  tumour  necrosis factor    alpha    (TNFa) in promonocytic U1 cells, increasing HIV-1 gene expression and inducing cytokine production and  thus  leading  to  upregulation  of  HIV  replication. The increased HIV production was inhibited by pentoxifylline, an  inhibitor of  TNFa  synthesis.  If  the  same  mechanism applies  equally  to  other  viruses,  dioxin  could  have increased the severity of infections such as rhinovirus and varicella among the St Kildans.Dioxin  accumulates  in  fatty  food  and  therefore  is augmented  in  the  human  food  chain.  Dioxins  and  the structurally   and   biologically   similar   polychlorinated biphenyls  (PCBs)  are  also  concentrated  in  breast  milk, giving a breast-feeding infant 80 times the intake of both toxic  chemical  groups  as  the  mother  on  a  weight-for-weight basis.28 Levels of PCBs are generally higher in the breast  milk  of  European  and  North American  women than   in  women from    non-industrialised countries. Guan found  higher  levels  of  three  dioxins  in  the  milk  of lactating  mothers  eating  a  lot  of  fatty  meat  and  fish,29precisely  the  diet  of  seabirds  and  fish  on  St  Kilda. Barraza-Vazquez30  demonstrated  a  positive  correlation between the maternal venous blood and umbilical cord levels    of    dichlorodiphenyltrichloroethane    (DDT). Kreuzer  tested  preschool  children  in  Michigan,  finding mean  serum  PCB  levels  of  0.3  ng/ml  in  bottle-fed children, compared with 5.1 ng/ml in children who were breast-fed for more than six months.31Therefore  a  pregnant  St  Kildan  woman,  living  in  her dioxin-contaminated  village,  would  have  exposed  her J R Coll Physicians Edinb 2009; 39:370–4© 2009 RCPEP Stride372history
fetus to minor amounts of dioxins in the mother’s serum and transferred via the placenta, followed by much higher levels of dioxin in breast milk. The effect on the immune system of children in the first few days of life is uncertain, but  one  can  speculate  that  this  may  have  been  a contributory factor to the neonatal tetanus problem.Clinical studiesThe   clinical   and   practical   significance   of   these epidemiological  data  and  in-vitro  studies,  and  of  the dioxin on St Kilda, is uncertain. The island did not have regular  medical  surveillance.  Haematological  and  bio-chemical   testing   was   unavailable.   Detailed   medical observation and analysis of the clinical effects of dioxins on  humans  and  their  infections  from  low-level  chronic toxicity  elsewhere  are  unavailable.  However,  comparison with  acute  dioxin  exposure  and  toxicity  elsewhere permits  an  assessment  of  the  severity  of  the  problem on St Kilda.The best-known episode of acute dioxin exposure is the Seveso  accident  near  Milan  in  1976,  when  an  aerosol cloud of TCDD and other chemicals was released over an 18-km2  area.32 The estimated amount of TCDD released was  34  kg,  compared  with  an  estimated  production  of 1  kg/year  in  the  Scottish  highlands  and  islands  in  the nineteenth  century.  The  area  surrounding  Seveso  was classified  into  three  zones,  A,  B  and  R,  with  decreasing levels of pollution. The levels of soil pollution and toxicity with dioxin compared with St Kilda are as follows:•  Seveso Zone    A: 15,500–580,000 ng/m2•  Seveso Zone    B: <5,000     ng/m2•  Seveso Zone    R: 1,500    ng/m2•  St    Kilda    peat: 114   ng/kg    (1  m3 dry soil = c. 700 kg)The  inhabitants  of  Seveso  were  extensively  investigated and  carefully  followed.  High  blood  levels  of TCDD  were found  but  no  significant  functional  abnormalities  in  the immunological,  hepatic,  neurological  and  reproductive systems. In the longer term, an increase in carcinomas of the  gastrointestinal  tract  and  lymphatic  systems  and  an increase in the death rate from respiratory disease were found. Exposed children in  Zone    A  showed higher    levels  of  complement  activity,  higher  values  for  lymphocyte responses to phytohaemagglutinin and pokeweed mitogen and increased numbers of peripheral lymphocytes. Chloracne  affected  a  significant  number  of  Seveso’s inhabitants, too. This is an acne-like skin eruption, most marked behind the ears, on the cheeks and in the axilla and groin. It is a very obvious rash and considered the classical   clinical   feature   of   dioxin   exposure.  Viktor Yushchenko,  the  President  of  Ukraine,  is  considered  to suffer  from  this  condition,  following  an  acute  illness  in 2004  in  which  his  face  became  disfigured,  scarred  and pockmarked. Levels of dioxin in his blood were reported to  be  6,000  times  above  the  safe  minimum,  but  the veracity of these tests are debated by toxicologists, and the  possibility  of  deliberate  poisoning  is  debated  in political circles.The St Kildan males grew heavy beards, but no pictures show  chloracne  in  women  or  children,  nor  are  there reports  from  visiting  doctors  or  other  sources  nor photos of axillary or inguinal rashes in men. Chloracne rates in the age group 3–14 years inhabiting Seveso and St Kilda were:•  Zone A: 42  of  214   children•  Zone B: 8  of  1,468    children•  Zone R: 63  of  8,680    children•  St    Kilda: none    documented and   none    visible    on  the many  photographs  of  the  island’s  children  from 1880–1930Another  smaller  but  relevant  chemical  leak  occurred from    a  BASF    trichlorophenol unit   in  Germany in  1953.33The   probable agent    was   also   TCDD.    Zober    published a 36-year follow-up of the exposed workers, and compared the   frequency of  disease with   that   of  BASF    workers not present  at  the  time  of  contamination  using  coded diagnoses   of   medical   insurance   claims.  Again,   the presence of chloracne was associated with higher levels of  blood  lipid  TCDD  concentrations.  The  calculated mean  blood  lipid  levels  were  1,118  parts  per  trillion (ppt) in the severe chloracne group and 148 ppt in the no-chloracne group.Thyroid disease and appendicitis were more common in the exposed group. Illness episodes were more common in the severe chloracne group than the reference group in each  of  the  periods  1953–59,  1960–69  and  1970–89. Acute  upper  respiratory  tract  infections  (URTIs)  were significantly  more  common  in  the  group  with  severe chloracne, at 15.8 per 100 person years, compared with 8.2 in the reference cohort. The rate was highest, at 18.8, in the severe chloracne group with TCDD levels of more than  1,000  ppt. There  was  a  non-significant  increase  in pneumonia and influenza in the severe chloracne group, as well  as  a  non-significant  increase  in  URTIs  in  those  with moderate or absent chloracne compared with the control group.  As  at  Seveso,  there  was  no  significant  health problem with those who did not have chloracne.conclusIonThe St Kildans were clearly potentially exposed to high levels of dioxin and were more susceptible to infections than  other  Hebridean  islanders.  However,  the  dioxin levels on the island were an order of magnitude below those found in the lightly polluted areas around Seveso, where  morbidity  and  mortality  were  not  affected,  and were even lower than in the highly polluted areas where clinical toxic effects were detected. Chloracne, the sine qua non of dioxin exposure, is not recorded on St Kilda. Dioxin, diet and disease on St KildaJ R Coll Physicians Edinb 2009; 39:370–4history© 2009 RCPE373
It  seems  only  possible  that  dioxins  on  the  island contributed   to   the   inhabitants’   morbidity,   in   the multifactorial  context  of  a  population  with  low  herd immunity,  malnutrition,  an  adverse  climate  and  limited immunological biodiversity.Depopulation in 1930 may have prevented the exposure of the islanders to increasing levels of dioxin and heavy metal in future years, with significant detectable clinical toxicity.


Nathan notes that the Anglicization used in the book 'Camps and Trails of Old China' is somewhat clunky, perhaps idiosyncratic, and likely describes places that do not have (or did not have at the time) standardized English forms.

Nathan notes that Roy Chapman Andrews’ orthography in 'Camps and Trails of Old China' often used single-syllable, hyphenated versions with very American spellings, such as 'Kwee-Yang' and 'Miow-Tsoo.'

Nathan is interested in exploring the idea of creating personality types based on physical reactions to emotional stimuli, such as yawning when nervous or feeling drowsy with the onset of certain substances. He recognizes that he tends to be emotionally distanced from interpersonal reactions unless they are extremely meaningful, at which point he may become flustered or oversensitive. Nathan is also somewhat insensitive to his own emotional states and tends to keep emotions at arm's length. He is curious about whether his perceptions of his own reactions are accurate and how one might develop a personality test or grouping based on these kinds of reactions. He is skeptical of traditional personality typing but finds this approach novel and intriguing.

Nathan uses iOS text shortcuts in Midjourney to select aspect ratios and is looking to create distinctive and memorable shortcut texts for common aspect ratios in cinema, print media, photography, and paper currency. He prefers aspect ratios to be expressed as non-decimal whole numbers when creating these shortcuts. Nathan also favors organized lists with format names, shortcut names, and aspect ratios presented in a consistent format. Currently, Nathan uses 'port' for —ar 9:16 and 'flat' for —ar 185:100, though 'flat' has been troublesome. Nathan is interested in creating a comprehensive list of aspect ratios for various media, with corresponding shortcut names and non-decimal whole number representations of the aspect ratios.

Nathan is interested in breaking down complex concepts into their simplest forms or 'lowest common denominators' and exploring how terms can be defined in relation to one another, particularly in the context of semantic or conceptual equations.
